Title: Colonization factors of human and animal-specific enterotoxigenic Escherichia coli (ETEC).
Journal: Trends in microbiology

Authors: von Mentzer A, Svennerholm AM

Published by Elsevier Ltd.</prism:publisher><dc:creator>von Mentzer, Astrid</dc:creator><dc:creator>Svennerholm, Ann-Mari</dc:creator><dc:description>
                  Colonization factors (CFs) are major virulence factors of enterotoxigenic Escherichia coli (ETEC). This pathogen is among the most common causes of bacterial diarrhea in children in low- and middle-income countries, travelers, and livestock. CFs are major candidate antigens in vaccines under development as preventive measures against ETEC infections in humans and livestock. Recent molecular studies have indicated that newly identified CFs on human ETEC are closely related to animal ETEC CFs. Increased knowledge of pathogenic mechanisms, immunogenicity, regulation, and expression of ETEC CFs, as well as the possible spread of animal ETEC to humans, may facilitate the future development of ETEC vaccines for humans and animals. Here, we present an updated review of CFs in ETEC.

            <ce:figure id="f0005">
               <ce:label>Figure 1</ce:label>
               <ce:caption id="ca0005">
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0005" view="all">Genetic organization of gene clusters encoding colonization factors (CFs).</ce:simple-para>
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0010" view="all">The open reading frames (ORFs) are colored based on the function of the protein. The gene clusters encoding the different CFs are highly diverse, but they all include a minimum set of genes: major subunit, minor subunit, chaperone, and outer membrane usher. Depending on the CFs, the order and the number of specific genes – that is, major subunits, minor subunits, and chaperones – can vary. Some gene clusters include a specific adhesin or tip protein, for example, the adhesins in Class Ib CFs and the tip protein CfaE in CFA/I. The gene clusters of CFs CS27A, CS27B, CS28A, and CS28B in Class Ib are not included as they are not published. The schematic figure was generated using R, specifically the gggenes package. Abbreviation: FUP, fimbrial usher protein.</ce:simple-para>
               </ce:caption>
               <ce:alt-text role="short" id="al0005">Figure 1</ce:alt-text>
               <ce:link id="lk0005" locator="gr1" xlink:type="simple" xlink:href="pii:S0966842X23003190/gr1" xlink:role="http://data.elsevier.com/vocabulary/ElsevierContentTypes/23.4"/>
            </ce:figure>
            <ce:figure id="f0010">
               <ce:label>Figure 2</ce:label>
               <ce:caption id="ca0010">
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0015" view="all">Relationship between human and animal ETEC colonization factors (CFs).</ce:simple-para>
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0020" view="all">A phylogenetic tree based on the protein sequences of the outer membrane ushers (fimbrial usher protein, FUP) shows that they cluster into three major FUP clusters: α (alpha), κ (kappa), and γ (gamma), including the subgroups γ2, γ3, and γ4. In the α-FUP cluster, the CFs CS5, and CS7, which are distantly located from other CFs in the α-FUP cluster, are found. The CS5 and CS7 operon structures differ from the rest of the ETEC CFs in the α-FUP cluster and, therefore, form a subcluster within the α-FUP cluster. The sequences encoding the ushers of CS15, CS22, CS27A, CS27B, CS28A, and CS28B are not available and, therefore, are not included in this phylogenetic tree. Nodes with a bootstrap value &gt;95 are marked with a red dot. The node leaves are colored based on the FUP cluster. A similar figure was published by Madhavan and Sakellaris in 2015 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0005" refid="bb0190">38</ce:cross-ref>]; here, additional sequences to include all published CFs from both human and animal ETEC CFs have been included. Accession numbers for each of the usher sequences used here are listed in <ce:cross-ref id="cf0010" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>. The phylogenetic tree was generated by first aligning the usher sequences using MAFFT v7.407 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0015" refid="bb0545">109</ce:cross-ref>], followed by running the maximum-likelihood algorithm (WAG+F+G4) with IQTree v2.2.0.3 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0020" refid="bb0550">110</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:simple-para>
               </ce:caption>
               <ce:alt-text role="short" id="al0010">Figure 2</ce:alt-text>
               <ce:link id="lk0010" locator="gr2" xlink:type="simple" xlink:href="pii:S0966842X23003190/gr2" xlink:role="http://data.elsevier.com/vocabulary/ElsevierContentTypes/23.4"/>
            </ce:figure>
            <ce:figure id="f0015">
               <ce:label>Figure 3</ce:label>
               <ce:caption id="ca0015">
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0025" view="all">Schematic representation of how chaperone–usher colonization factors are assembled.</ce:simple-para>
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0030" view="all">The colonization factor (CF) subunits are secreted through the Sec translocase. In the periplasm, chaperones bind to subunits and catalyze their folding. The chaperone–subunit complex makes its way to the outer-membrane usher, which secretes the assembled subunits across the outer membrane [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0025" refid="bb0195">39</ce:cross-ref>]. Figure created with <ce:inter-ref id="ir0005" xlink:href="http://BioRender.com" xlink:type="simple">BioRender.com</ce:inter-ref>
                  </ce:simple-para>
               </ce:caption>
               <ce:alt-text role="short" id="al0015">Figure 3</ce:alt-text>
               <ce:link id="lk0015" locator="gr3" xlink:type="simple" xlink:href="pii:S0966842X23003190/gr3" xlink:role="http://data.elsevier.com/vocabulary/ElsevierContentTypes/23.4"/>
            </ce:figure>

            <ce:textbox id="b0005">
               <ce:label>Box 1</ce:label>
               <ce:caption id="ca0030">
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0045" view="all">Terminology of ETEC CFs</ce:simple-para>
               </ce:caption>
               <ce:alt-text role="short" id="al0030">Box 1</ce:alt-text>
               <ce:textbox-body>
                  <ce:sections>
                     <ce:para id="p0005" view="all">Previously, the terminology for human ETEC CFs was somewhat confusing but simplified and organized as presented by Gaastra and Svennerholm [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0400" refid="bb0570">114</ce:cross-ref>]; their terminology has been used in most publications of ETEC CFs thereafter. Previously the CFs were named CFA (Colonization Factor Antigen) followed by a roman number, for example, CFA/I, CFA/II, which was later identified as three distinct CFs: CS1, CS2, and CS3 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0405" refid="bb0575">115</ce:cross-ref>], and CFA/IV: CS4, CS5 and CS6. The first CF, CFA/I, has kept its old designation. Now, human ETEC CFs are given a CS (Coli Surface antigen) designation followed by a number, for example, CS1 and CS2. The order of naming CFs proposed in 1996 is still valid and is recommended to be used for new CFs identified based on the order of identification. However, naming genes encoding the CF has yet to be similarly systematized. Some of the previous designations are still used, for example, CS8 and CS21, which belong to the class type 4 pilus, are sometimes referred to as CFA/III and Longus, respectively. The CFs in animal ETEC are generally referred to as fimbriae (F). As mentioned already, the human ETEC CFs are further classified based on the structure: nonfimbrial, fimbriae, or fibrillae. However, animal Fs are not categorized based on their structure – all are referred to as fimbriae, although four of six have fibrillar structure [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0410" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>] (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0415" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>). In this review both CFs and Fs are referred to as colonization factors (CFs). The old designations for animal CFs, that is, K88, K99, 987P, are still used in recent publications.</ce:para>
                  </ce:sections>
               </ce:textbox-body>
            </ce:textbox>
         </ce:floats>

            <ce:abstract id="ab0010" xml:lang="en" view="all" class="author"><ce:abstract-sec id="as0010" view="all">
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0055" view="all">Colonization factors (CFs) are major virulence factors of enterotoxigenic <ce:italic>Escherichia coli</ce:italic> (ETEC). This pathogen is among the most common causes of bacterial diarrhea in children in low- and middle-income countries, travelers, and livestock. CFs are major candidate antigens in vaccines under development as preventive measures against ETEC infections in humans and livestock. Recent molecular studies have indicated that newly identified CFs on human ETEC are closely related to animal ETEC CFs. Increased knowledge of pathogenic mechanisms, immunogenicity, regulation, and expression of ETEC CFs, as well as the possible spread of animal ETEC to humans, may facilitate the future development of ETEC vaccines for humans and animals. Here, we present an updated review of CFs in ETEC.</ce:simple-para>
               </ce:abstract-sec></ce:abstract>
            <ce:abstract xml:lang="en" id="ab0015" class="teaser" view="all">
               <ce:abstract-sec id="as0015" view="all">
                  <ce:simple-para id="sp0060" view="all">Colonization factors (CFs) are major virulence factors of enterotoxigenic <ce:italic>Escherichia coli</ce:italic> (ETEC). This pathogen is among the most common causes of bacterial diarrhea in children in low- and middle-income countries, travelers, and livestock. CFs are major candidate antigens in vaccines under development as preventive measures against ETEC infections in humans and livestock. Recent molecular studies have indicated that newly identified CFs on human ETEC are closely related to animal ETEC CFs. Increased knowledge of pathogenic mechanisms, immunogenicity, regulation, and expression of ETEC CFs, as well as the possible spread of animal ETEC to humans, may facilitate the future development of ETEC vaccines for humans and animals. Here, we present an updated review of CFs in ETEC.</ce:simple-para>
               </ce:abstract-sec>
            </ce:abstract>

         <body view="all">
            <ce:sections>
               <ce:section id="s0005" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0015">Introduction</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0035" view="all">Diarrheal pathogens are a leading cause of morbidity and mortality globally [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0440" refid="bb0005">1</ce:cross-ref>], with <ce:bold>enterotoxigenic <ce:italic>Escherichia coli</ce:italic>
                     </ce:bold> (<ce:bold>ETEC</ce:bold>) (see <ce:cross-ref refid="gs0005" id="cf0445">Glossary</ce:cross-ref>) accounting for up to 220 million cases of diarrhea each year in resource-poor countries [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0450" refid="bb0010">2</ce:cross-ref>]. Children under five years of age are the most vulnerable group, but ETEC can also cause disease in adults and is the major cause of diarrhea in travelers [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0455" refid="bb0015">3</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0040" view="all">ETEC causes diarrhea in livestock, including pigs, cattle, and sheep [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0460" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>]. <ce:bold>Post-weaning diarrhea (PWD)</ce:bold> is a complex disease caused mainly by ETEC. Newly weaned piglets are susceptible to PWD, which remains a significant cause of illness in pigs and accounts for substantial global economic losses [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0465" refid="bb0025">5</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0045" view="all">ETEC colonizes the small intestine of humans and animals, particularly piglets and calves, by adhering to intestinal epithelial cells by <ce:bold>colonization factors</ce:bold> (<ce:bold>CFs</ce:bold>). ETEC must overcome the harsh environment in the stomach and combat host defenses, such as antibacterial peptides, mucin, secretory IgA, and epithelial shedding, in order to colonize. After successfully colonizing the small intestine by binding to receptors and different surface structures, the bacteria initiate the production and secretion of <ce:bold>heat-labile toxin</ce:bold> (<ce:bold>LT</ce:bold>), <ce:bold>heat-stable toxin</ce:bold> (<ce:bold>ST</ce:bold>), or both <ce:bold>entero</ce:bold>
                     <ce:bold>toxins</ce:bold>, which cause diarrhea. There are two types of ST, STa and STb; the two subtypes of STa, STp and STh, are found in ETEC isolated from humans and livestock; in contrast, STb has been found only in ETEC infecting livestock [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0470" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>]. CFs and enterotoxins are considered the main virulence factors of ETEC. Still, during recent years, different <ce:bold>noncanonical antigens</ce:bold>, for example, EtpA (two-partner secretion exoprotein) and EatA (serine protease), have been proposed as additional putative virulence factors [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0475" refid="bb0030">6</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0480" refid="bb0035">7</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0050" view="all">This review aims to present current knowledge regarding the genomics, prevalence, and role of CFs in human and animal-associated ETEC pathogenesis and the importance of CFs in vaccine development.</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0010" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0020">Prevalence and epidemiology of CFs in ETEC</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0055" view="all">Human ETEC exhibits remarkable heterogeneity. In addition to producing LT, ST, or both toxins, it can express at least 128 different O antigens, several other K and H antigens [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0485" refid="bb0040">8</ce:cross-ref>], and produce one or more of the at least 29 different CFs identified so far. Attempts to identify the most common serotypes in ETEC strains as a basis for further characterization and vaccine development were previously undertaken with limited success based on the significant variation of <ce:italic>
                        <ce:bold>Escherichia coli</ce:bold>
                     </ce:italic> (<ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic>) serogroups and serotypes [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0490" refid="bb0040">8</ce:cross-ref>]. Instead, a search for the most prevalent CFs in ETEC was more successful for vaccine development, resulting in the identification of CFA/I and CS1–CS6 as the most common CFs, constituting 60–80% of all CFs expressed by clinical ETEC isolates in different geographic areas and populations [<ce:cross-refs id="cf0495" refid="bb0045 bb0050 bb0055 bb0060">9–12</ce:cross-refs>]. Several studies have also identified additional CFs common in certain areas and during outbreaks, for example, CS7, CS14, and CS17 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0500" refid="bb0045">9</ce:cross-ref>]. The ETEC toxin profiles are often combined with specific CFs, for example, CFA/I and ST only, LT/ST with CS1+CS3, or CS2+CS3. ETEC strains are also prevalent in certain populations, for example, STp CS6 strains are frequently identified in travelers. Still, they are less common as a cause of diarrhea in children in endemic areas [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0505" refid="bb0065">13</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0510" refid="bb0070">14</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0060" view="all">Diarrhea remains a significant global concern in livestock, leading to high mortality and morbidity rates in pigs. Among the causative pathogens, ETEC is considered one of the most common. ETEC strains that infect livestock typically express one of six known CFs. Notably, F4 (K88) and F18 (F107) ETEC strains are the most prevalent and are frequently associated with PWD. Conversely, F5 (K99), F6 (987P), and F41a ETEC are primarily associated with neonatal diarrhea [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0515" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0520" refid="bb0075">15</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0525" refid="bb0080">16</ce:cross-ref>], while F17-positive strains are commonly associated with diseases in calves of various and other livestock animals such as lambs and alpacas [<ce:cross-refs id="cf0530" refid="bb0085 bb0090 bb0095">17–19</ce:cross-refs>]. It is worth noting that F4 ETEC can also cause disease in neonatal piglets [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0535" refid="bb0100">20</ce:cross-ref>]. There is a growing concern regarding the potential increase in cases of PWD and neonatal diarrhea following the ban on the use of zinc oxide [<ce:cross-refs id="cf0540" refid="bb0105 bb0110 bb0115">21–23</ce:cross-refs>], especially when considering the concurrent use of antibiotics.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0065" view="all">Efforts have been made to identify specific combinations of different O antigens and CFs [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0545" refid="bb0040">8</ce:cross-ref>]. However, these have not been extensively pursued in recent years due to the scarcity of simple methods for identifying O antigens. Furthermore, while most animal-specific CFs exhibit temporal and geographic variations, recently isolated CF-positive ETEC strains demonstrate less diverse CF and O-serotype profiles than those from the 1970s and 1980s [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0550" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>]. This could be due to selective pressures related to vaccination strategies, the genetic makeup of pigs, as well as changes in the environment in pig farms today.</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0015" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0025">Human-specific colonization factors</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0070" view="all">CFs are key factors in ETEC pathogenesis as they are proteinaceous surface structures that bind to the intestinal cell surface. Currently, 29 human ETEC CFs have been identified (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0555" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:float-anchor refid="t0005"/>). The CFs are divided into groups based on specific attributes, most commonly genetic and antigenic relatedness. Currently, there are four main CF groups: CFA/I-like, CS5-like, and the Class1b group, as well as a more diverse group of CFs. The best-characterized CFs are CFA/I, CS1–CS6 and CS21 (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0560" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>). ETEC isolates typically express one, two, or three CFs, and several conserved CF and toxin profiles have been identified [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0565" refid="bb0120">24</ce:cross-ref>]. For example, CS1+CS3 LT+STh, CS2+CS3 LT+STh, CS5+CS6 LT+STh, CS6 STp, CFA/I STh, CS7 LT, and CS17 LT have been isolated worldwide from patients with diarrhea [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0570" refid="bb0045">9</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0575" refid="bb0125">25</ce:cross-ref>]. However, the Class 1b CFs (CS12, CS18, CS20, CS26, CS27, CS28, CS30, and F6), as well as CS13 and CS23 (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0580" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>), are thought to be less prevalent than the CFA/I-like group (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0585" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>), and they are not screened for in clinical isolates to the same extent as the more prevalent CFs [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0590" refid="bb0045">9</ce:cross-ref>]. A recent study of ETEC epidemiology in La Paz, Bolivia, revealed that CS23 was isolated from both patients and environmental samples (water). Five distinct CS23 variants were identified [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0595" refid="bb0130">26</ce:cross-ref>], highlighting our limited understanding of the variation within CF gene clusters.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0075" view="all">ETEC isolates can also express two different Type IV pili, CS8 and CS21, which are similar to each other in terms of both gene cluster organization and sequence similarity, where the major subunits, LngA (CS21) and CofA (CS8), share 79% of their amino acid sequence (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0605" refid="f0005">Figure 1</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:float-anchor refid="f0005"/>). The CS21 operon also includes two additional genes, lngX1 and lngX2, with unknown functions (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0610" refid="f0005">Figure 1</ce:cross-ref>). CS21, one of the most prevalent CFs in ETEC, is primarily found in ETEC strains with other CFs, for example, CFA/I, CS1+CS3, CS2+CS3, CS6, or CS6+CS8 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0615" refid="bb0120">24</ce:cross-ref>]. Some studies have indicated that CS21 (Longus) may mediate adherence [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0620" refid="bb0135">27</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0625" refid="bb0140">28</ce:cross-ref>]; in contrast, Smith <ce:italic>et al.</ce:italic> show that antibodies blocking CS21 did not significantly decrease the adherence of a CFA/I+CS21 wild-type ETEC strain [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0630" refid="bb0145">29</ce:cross-ref>]. Furthermore, since CS21 is rarely found on its own and is mostly coexpressed with, for example, CFA/I, CS6, and CS3, together with CS1 or CS2 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0635" refid="bb0045">9</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0640" refid="bb0150">30</ce:cross-ref>], its role in ETEC pathogenesis is difficult to evaluate.</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0020" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0030">Animal-specific CFs</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0080" view="all">Animal ETECs are less heterogeneous than human ETECs regarding the CF virulence factors. Six clinically relevant CFs – F4, F5, F6, F41a, F17, and F18 – have been identified in ETEC-caused disease in pigs in which F4 and F18 strains are commonly associated with PWD, but F4 ETEC can also cause neonatal diarrhea since the F4 receptors are expressed from birth whereas F18 receptors are not fully expressed until the piglet reach 20 days of age [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0645" refid="bb0155">31</ce:cross-ref>]. The CFs F5, F6, F17, and F41a ETEC are associated with neonatal diarrhea [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0650" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0655" refid="bb0075">15</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0660" refid="bb0090">18</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0665" refid="bb0160">32</ce:cross-ref>] (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0670" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0085" view="all">Additional animal CFs have been reported, but their functionality and role in ETEC disease are inconclusive. For example, F42 has been shown to share genetic and immunological determinants with F41a [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0675" refid="bb0165">33</ce:cross-ref>]. F165, encoded by the <ce:italic>foo</ce:italic> operon, has been identified in <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> O115 strains isolated from piglets and calves suffering from diarrhea, and calves, piglets, and poultry with septicemia [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0680" refid="bb0170">34</ce:cross-ref>]. The association between F165 and ETEC has not been determined. It is unclear whether samples from piglets and calves with diarrhea have been screened for F165 and F42. Furthermore, CS31A, identified on ETEC infecting calves, is genetically related to the porcine-related ETEC F4 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0685" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0090" view="all">Previously, it was thought that porcine ETEC has a diverse chromosomal background with no predominant clone or clonal group. However, a later study – based on phylogenetic analysis of multilocus sequence type (MLST) alleles – shows that most F4 and F18-positive ETEC isolates cluster together in a subset of <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> lineages [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0690" refid="bb0175">35</ce:cross-ref>]. In total, 15 different O antigens have been described in clinical ETEC isolates from animals, and nine of these are also found in human ETEC isolates [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0695" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0700" refid="bb0180">36</ce:cross-ref>] (see <ce:cross-ref id="cf7000" refid="b0005">Box 1</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:float-anchor refid="b0005"/>).</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0025" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0035">Genomic insights into CFs</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0095" view="all">Previously, human-specific ETEC CFs were grouped into families based on shared antigenic properties and the structure of the CFs. However, developing a more robust classification scheme of the CFs by sequence comparisons and phylogenetic analysis to decipher the ancestral evolution of both conserved and more diverse genes is more reliable. For example, protein sequence comparison of the conserved outer-membrane usher places CFs into fimbrial usher protein (FUP) clusters (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0705" refid="f0010">Figure 2</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:float-anchor refid="f0010"/>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0100" view="all">ETEC CFs are organized in gene clusters, that is, operons, and the structure of the gene clusters is conserved across the FUP clusters with some exceptions. Most CF operons are flanked by insertion sequence (IS) elements, suggesting that CF operons have been mobile by transposition (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0710" refid="f0005">Figure 1</ce:cross-ref>). The CF operons also have a much lower GC content than known <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> genes, indicating a different origin of the operon [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0715" refid="bb0185">37</ce:cross-ref>]. The CF operons differ in the number of genes and the organization of the genes. For example, the α-FUP CF operons (CFA/I-like) have one chaperone, whereas CS5 and CS7 have two chaperones, and γ<ce:inf loc="post">2</ce:inf>-FUP (Class 1b) has three chaperones. Each chaperone in γ<ce:inf loc="post">2</ce:inf>-FUP operons binds to a specific fimbrial subunit (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0720" refid="f0015">Figure 3</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:float-anchor refid="f0015"/>).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0105" view="all">Most ETEC CFs are assembled by the chaperone–usher pathway. The assembly includes chaperones promoting correct folding and maturation of subunit proteins (including major and minor subunits, adhesins/tip proteins) in the periplasm. The chaperones also prevent premature polymerization of the subunits and transport the subunits to the outer-membrane usher [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0725" refid="bb0190">38</ce:cross-ref>] (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0730" refid="f0015">Figure 3</ce:cross-ref>). The ETEC CFs are classified as helical, fimbrial, fibrillar, or afimbrial (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0735" refid="t0005">Table 1</ce:cross-ref>); this is often determined by electron microscopy and predictions based on the protein sequence. The most studied chaperone-usher-assembled pili are the P-fimbriae and type I fimbriae (analyzed mainly for urinary tract <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic>) [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0740" refid="bb0195">39</ce:cross-ref>]. Type I fimbriae are distant relatives to ETEC chaperone-usher assembled CFs (belonging to γ<ce:inf loc="post">1</ce:inf>-FUP) (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0745" refid="f0010">Figure 2</ce:cross-ref>). The functionality or assembly of ETEC CFs is not well studied but instead relies on the high similarity of other, more well-studied chaperone-usher pili.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0110" view="all">Interestingly, two clades of FUPs, namely the γ2-FUP and κ-FUP clades, include colonization factors identified in ETEC strains isolated from human and animal hosts, distinguishing them from the other FUP clades. Genomic analysis of gene clusters encoding known ETEC CFs shows that the level of sequence diversity varies depending on the FUP clade [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0750" refid="bb0190">38</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0115" view="all">Based on detailed studies on a few ETEC isolates, it is assumed that genes encoding the majority of human ETEC CFs are located on plasmids, except CS2, which is located on the chromosome [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0755" refid="bb0185">37</ce:cross-ref>]. Plasmids may also carry a partial (e.g., plasmids pAvM_E925_4 [CS3] and pAvM_E925_6 [CS1]) or complete transfer (<ce:italic>tra</ce:italic>) loci (pAvM_E925_5 [CS21]) enabling the horizontal transfer of plasmids [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0760" refid="bb0185">37</ce:cross-ref>]. Furthermore, in the ETEC reference strain E925 (LT+ STh CS1+CS3, O6), plasmids pAvM_E925_6 and pAvM_E925_7 are similar to pCoo, a cointegrate plasmid, indicating that CS1 can be horizontally transferred [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0765" refid="bb0185">37</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0770" refid="bb0200">40</ce:cross-ref>]. The horizontal transfer of plasmids with CFA/I [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0775" refid="bb0205">41</ce:cross-ref>] and CS1 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0780" refid="bb0210">42</ce:cross-ref>] has been confirmed, but additional studies on how these plasmids are transferred are needed to understand ETEC's evolution further. Moreover, additional in-depth analysis of the genomic content of ETEC isolates from different ecological niches is needed to further understand the location and movement of virulence genes.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0120" view="all">Most studies on CF prevalence have focused on individual hosts, whereas the prevalence across multiple hosts is unknown. However, related CFs could indicate that some factors can recognize receptors in the intestine of multiple hosts, such as humans and pigs. Recent evidence indicates the potential for ETEC being capable of host-jumping. For example, the CF CS30, found in ETEC infecting humans, is related to porcine F6 and can recognize carbohydrate structures in both human and pig intestines [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0785" refid="bb0215">43</ce:cross-ref>]. Another example is that specific ETEC strains expressing F18, CFA/I-positive, or the noncanonical virulence factor EtpA [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0790" refid="bb0220">44</ce:cross-ref>], recognize blood group sugar structures as receptors. Additionally, phylogenetic overlap, and similar virulence traits, have been observed in ETEC isolated from humans and swine [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0795" refid="bb0225">45</ce:cross-ref>]. However, further investigation is warranted to understand the extent of host-jumping of ETEC as it has been shown that exposure to enteric pathogens from animals, combined with well-known water and sanitation risk factors, poses a threat to human health [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0800" refid="bb0230">46</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0125" view="all">Additional genomic-based studies examining human and animal-specific ETEC could be instrumental in addressing CF diversity, as current knowledge in this area remains limited. These findings would not only improve our understanding of ETEC diversity, but also potentially lead to the development of more effective prevention strategies for diarrheal diseases in both humans and animals (see <ce:cross-ref id="cf7005" refid="b0010">Outstanding questions</ce:cross-ref>).</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0030" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0040">Intestinal receptors and binding structures</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0130" view="all">The binding specificities of ETEC CFs in the human intestine have been less studied than the CFs expressed by strains of ETEC that infect pigs. Receptors identified for the most important CFs are described in the following sections and in <ce:cross-ref id="cf0805" refid="t0010">Table 2</ce:cross-ref>
                     <ce:float-anchor refid="t0010"/>.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0135" view="all">The major subunit CfaB of CFA/I has been shown to bind to an extensive array of glycosphingolipids and glycoproteins present in substantial amounts in the human small intestine [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0810" refid="bb0235">47</ce:cross-ref>]. Moreover, studies have reported that CfaB can bind to blood group Lewis a (Le<ce:sup loc="post">a</ce:sup>) antigen, a glycan that is ubiquitously expressed in the small intestine of children &lt;2 years of age and in individuals with a mutation in the FUT2 gene [<ce:cross-refs id="cf0815" refid="bb0240 bb0245 bb0250 bb0255">48–51</ce:cross-refs>]. Previously, CS1 through CS4 were shown to bind to sialic-acid-containing glycoconjugates and asialogangliosides (asialo-GM1) [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0820" refid="bb0260">52</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0825" refid="bb0265">53</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0140" view="all">CS6, with two major subunits, CssA and CssB, is a polyvalent adhesin capable of attaching to several binding structures. CssB binds to sulfatide and recognizes the extracellular matrix protein fibronectin, and CssA shows general adhesiveness [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0830" refid="bb0270">54</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0145" view="all">For the recently identified CF CS30, which was found to be related to the porcine ETEC F6 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0835" refid="bb0275">55</ce:cross-ref>], sulfatide was identified as a candidate receptor for the adhesion of CS30-positive ETEC to both human and porcine small intestinal cells [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0840" refid="bb0215">43</ce:cross-ref>]. F6 was also shown to bind to two specific glycosphingolipids, lactotriasylceramide and lactotetraosylceramide, present in the porcine mucosa [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0845" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>] (see Outstanding questions).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0150" view="all">Carbohydrate intestinal receptors for porcine ETEC have been thoroughly investigated, for example, the tip adhesin FedF of F18 interacts with blood group sugars expressed on intestinal epithelial cells (<ce:cross-ref id="cf0850" refid="t0010">Table 2</ce:cross-ref>) [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0855" refid="bb0280">56</ce:cross-ref>], and the tree F4 subtypes ab, ac, and ad recognize both acid and non-acid glycosphingolipids [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0860" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0035" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0045">Regulators of CFs</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0155" view="all">The regulation of CFs in ETEC is a complex process involving environmental and genetic factors. While there is no common regulation for all CFs, specific regulators that respond to environmental cues – such as temperature, osmolarity, oxygen level, pH, bile, and mucin – have been identified.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0160" view="all">Notably, many human and animal CFs are upregulated at 37°C compared to 20°C, where their expression is significantly lower or absent. H-NS, a global gene regulator involved in the control of environmentally responsive genes, acts as a negative regulator for CFA/I [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0865" refid="bb0285">57</ce:cross-ref>], CS1 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0870" refid="bb0285">57</ce:cross-ref>], and CS21 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0875" refid="bb0290">58</ce:cross-ref>]. However, the expression of CS21 (Longus) is downregulated by H-NS and CRP but upregulated via CpxR in the presence of glucose [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0880" refid="bb0290">58</ce:cross-ref>]. H-NS may also regulate AraC-family regulators that control ETEC CFs [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0885" refid="bb0295">59</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0165" view="all">Furthermore, CFA/I-positive ETEC respond to environmental oxygen levels through the nitrate reduction regulator (FNR), leading to derepression of genes near epithelial cells where oxygen levels are low [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0890" refid="bb0300">60</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0895" refid="bb0305">61</ce:cross-ref>]. Interestingly, mucin, secreted from epithelial cells as a protectant agent, can also serve as a carbohydrate source for bacteria. Notably, it has been observed that mucin can upregulate the expression of CFA/I, CS1, and CS3 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0900" refid="bb0310">62</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0170" view="all">Recent investigations have uncovered additional factors influencing CF expression in human ETEC. For instance, iron was recently shown to increase the expression of CFA/I and CS6 via IscR [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0905" refid="bb0315">63</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0910" refid="bb0320">64</ce:cross-ref>]. Moreover, CFA/I is upregulated by ethanolamine, likely involving the sensor kinase QseE [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0915" refid="bb0325">65</ce:cross-ref>]. A recent study exploring the effects of multiple environmental cues on the transcriptional control of CS3 revealed that the transcription of the major subunit (CstH) was positively regulated by temperature (37°C) and glycerol. Conversely, CstH was repressed by glucose and the depletion of divalent cations, such as calcium and magnesium. H-NS and CprxRA act as repressors, while CRP activates CS3 expression [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0920" refid="bb0330">66</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0175" view="all">Bile plays a critical role in upregulating the expression of several ETEC CFs, including CS5, CS7, CS8, CS14, and CS17 involving AraC-regulators (CfaD/R, CsvR, and Rns) [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0925" refid="bb0335">67</ce:cross-ref>], with Na-glycocholate hydrate responsible for the upregulation of CS5 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0930" refid="bb0340">68</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0935" refid="bb0345">69</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0180" view="all">Multiple homologous AraC regulators, including CfaD, CsvR, and Rns, have been identified. CfaD/CfaR positively regulates the CFA/I gene cluster, for example, in the presence of glucose [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0940" refid="bb0350">70</ce:cross-ref>], as well as other CFs belonging to the α-FUP (CS4, CS12, CS19, and CS20), while CsvR regulates CS5 and probably CS7. Rns positively regulates CS1, CS2, and CS14 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0945" refid="bb0285">57</ce:cross-ref>]. However, little is known about the regulation of animal-specific CFs, except for F6, which is positively regulated by the AraC regulator FapR [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0950" refid="bb0020">4</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0185" view="all">Phase variation regulates some CFs, for example, CS18 and possibly the related CS30. CS18 and CS30 both have two genes encoding site-specific recombinases located upstream of the CF gene clusters, similar to the regulatory mechanism of type I pili [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0955" refid="bb0275">55</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0960" refid="bb0285">57</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0965" refid="bb0355">71</ce:cross-ref>]. The two site-specific recombinases, FotS and FotT, catalyze the inversion of a 312 bp inversion that contains a predicted promoter and is located just upstream of the gene encoding the major subunit (FotA) [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0970" refid="bb0355">71</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0040" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0050">Diagnostics to identify ETEC CFs</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0190" view="all">Inactivated enterotoxins and CFs are leading vaccine candidates; therefore, surveilling potential shifts in CF profiles in different geographic locations and populations over time is essential. Since the early 2000s, initial methods such as hemagglutination, serum agglutination, and immunodiffusion assays have been replaced by gene probes [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0975" refid="bb0360">72</ce:cross-ref>] and dot blot assays demonstrating reactivity with specific anti-CF monoclonal antibodies on nitrocellulose sheets [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0980" refid="bb0365">73</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0985" refid="bb0370">74</ce:cross-ref>]. Later, phenotypic methods have been replaced or combined with PCR methods, that is, conventional or multiplex PCR using specific primers now available for most identified human and animal ETEC CFs [<ce:cross-ref id="cf0990" refid="bb0045">9</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf0995" refid="bb0335">67</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1000" refid="bb0375">75</ce:cross-ref>]. Most PCR primers target the major subunits that are the most variable genes in CF gene clusters. In recent years, the use of real-time (RT) PCR [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1005" refid="bb0380">76</ce:cross-ref>], TaqMan [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1010" refid="bb0385">77</ce:cross-ref>], or Luminex [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1015" refid="bb0390">78</ce:cross-ref>] analyses has been introduced for large-scale ETEC CFs in diagnostic and epidemiological studies. A sequencing approach to identify key virulence factors and antibiotic-resistance determinants may be used for diagnostic purposes [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1020" refid="bb0395">79</ce:cross-ref>]; however, sequencing is still inaccessible in many countries where ETEC is a health problem.</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0045" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0055">Methodologies to identify and characterize new CFs</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0195" view="all">New CFs can be identified by binding ETEC strains to specific cell lines, intestinal biopsies, or genetic approaches, including forward or reverse genetics. Many CFs identified and characterized are thermoregulated, that is, expressed at 37°C but not at 20°C. Using a reverse genetics approach, whereby sequencing data is used for identifying potential new genes and gene clusters that share similarities with known CFs, new CFs may be determined. Identification of gene clusters that may encode putative CFs can be made using pure bioinformatics [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1025" refid="bb0400">80</ce:cross-ref>]. However, several gene clusters encoding putative adhesins are present in <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> (both commensal and pathogenic <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic>) but are not expressed [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1030" refid="bb0405">81</ce:cross-ref>]. One approach to identifying and characterizing new colonization factors involves integrating results from bioinformatic analysis with functional studies. For instance, putative CF gene clusters can be identified with comparative genomics and molecular weight of the major subunit, which has been predicted to be between 12 and 18 kDa. The functionality of the predicted gene clusters is subsequently evaluated. Most CFs are thermoregulated; therefore, selected strains, including potential knockout strains lacking the major subunit, can be grown at 37°C and 20°C to identify those that express a protein with a predicted molecular weight of the putative major subunit only at 37°C by sodium dodecyl sulfate–polyacrylamide gel electrophoresis (SDS-PAGE). This could be complemented by the capacity of ETEC expressing the putative CF to adhere to intestinal cells, for example, Caco-2 (human) or IPEC-J2 (porcine) cell or intestinal biopsies using bacteria cultured at 37°C and 20°C, respectively; binding by bacteria culture at 37°C supports identification of a functional new CF [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1035" refid="bb0275">55</ce:cross-ref>]. Although the listed cell lines (Caco-2 and IPEC-J2) are widely used, options to use a model system that better resembles the small intestine could be advantageous, for example, organoids or small intestinal organoids, also called enteroids. Organoids are derived from induced pluripotent stem cells (iPSCs) and contain cells of epithelial and mesenchymal lineages. Enteroids are derived from intestinal tissue and contain cells of epithelial lineage only; for ETEC–host interactions, small intestinal enteroids would be preferred [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1040" refid="bb0410">82</ce:cross-ref>]. Enteroids have recently been extensively used for studying host–ETEC interactions, both using human and pig-derived enteroids [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1045" refid="bb0145">29</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-refs id="cf1050" refid="bb0415 bb0420 bb0425">83–85</ce:cross-refs>]. Clustered regularly interspaced short palindromic repeats (CRISPR)-Cas9 technology can be used to edit both systems, allowing host–pathogen interactions to be more closely analyzed [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1055" refid="bb0430">86</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0200" view="all">It should be noted that some ETEC strains express type I fimbriae or other adhesins and need to be considered when investigating putative new CFs. If possible, bioinformatic analysis can identify strains that lack the type I fimbriae but are positive for the putative CF, and this would be a good reference strain for experimental analysis or simply by screening strains using PCR. Moreover, adding mannose in culture media during adherence assays blocks the binding of type I fimbriae. Lastly, electron microscopy can be used to visualize the structure of the putative CF [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1060" refid="bb0275">55</ce:cross-ref>] (see Outstanding questions).</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0050" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0060">Immune responses</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0205" view="all">ETEC CFs are strong immunogens after disease and vaccination in humans and animals. Thus, patients with CF-positive ETEC disease develop robust mucosal and systemic immune responses to CFs of the infecting ETEC strain [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1065" refid="bb0125">25</ce:cross-ref>]. Oral administration of CF-positive ETEC vaccines has induced significant mucosal immune responses against the vaccine CFs in a majority of vaccinated infants, children, and adults [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1070" refid="bb0435">87</ce:cross-ref>]. Specifically, the tip-proteins/adhesins of CFA/I, CS1, CS2, CS4, and CS17 have been shown to not only be involved in binding to intestinal cells but also to function as protective immunogens [<ce:cross-refs id="cf1075" refid="bb0440 bb0445 bb0450">88–90</ce:cross-refs>]. The CFs may also induce long-lasting immunological memory, as observed both after clinical infection and oral vaccination [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1080" refid="bb0245">49</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1085" refid="bb0455">91</ce:cross-ref>]. Parenteral (intradermal, transcutaneous, intramuscular, etc.) administration of CF-based vaccine constructs has also induced highly significant immune responses in serum, whereas mucosal immune responses have been weaker [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1090" refid="bb0460">92</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1095" refid="bb0465">93</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0210" view="all">Vaccinating sows before farrowing is a vaccination strategy to protect neonatal piglets, which stimulates the sows' mucosal immunity and elicits specific IgG and IgA antibodies in the colostrum, conferring protection to the neonatal piglets. However, the parental vaccines to prevent PWD stimulate the systemic rather than the mucosal immune system. ETEC strains expressing F4 or F18 fimbriae are the most commonly associated with diarrhea in weaned pigs. Significant correlations have been observed between F4 or F18 fimbrial adhesins and pathogenicity. However, variations in host response have also been documented following infection with either F4 or F18 ETEC. These variations include differences in immune response patterns, the duration and severity of symptoms such as piglet diarrhea, and the timing of intermittent bacterial shedding [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1100" refid="bb0470">94</ce:cross-ref>]. However, studies have shown that F4-based parental vaccine formulations can induce a systemic serum IgA response. For example, a three-component vaccine with FaeG (F4), FedF (F18), and LT fusion protein induced IgA antibodies, which could be detected in serum, feces, and intestinal washes [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1105" refid="bb0475">95</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0215" view="all">Many human ETEC CFs are closely related, that is, cross-reactive epitopes have been identified within the CFA/I and CS5 families, respectively; in particular, the CF tip proteins are highly conserved. An oral inactivated ETEC vaccine containing CFA/I and CS5 ETEC induced significant mucosal immune responses against the CFA/I related CS14 and CS17 CFs and the CS5-related CS7 in a majority of adult Swedes [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1110" refid="bb0480">96</ce:cross-ref>] as well as in most of the immunized children and adults in Bangladesh [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1115" refid="bb0485">97</ce:cross-ref>]. Furthermore, ETVAX vaccine-induced antibodies' cross-reactivity also shows strong IgG responses against the CF PCFO71 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1120" refid="bb0490">98</ce:cross-ref>]. It has been demonstrated that porcine F4/F18 oral vaccine protects piglets in challenge studies with F4+ and F18+ ETEC; however, they are not cross-protecting against F5, F6, or F41 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1125" refid="bb0495">99</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0055" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0065">Vaccines</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0220" view="all">In the development of ETEC vaccines, strong evidence supports the potential for effective vaccines based on CFs. Candidate vaccines based on live or killed CF-expressing ETEC or purified CF components have induced strong systemic and/or mucosal immune responses against the vaccine CFs [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1130" refid="bb0435">87</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1135" refid="bb0485">97</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1140" refid="bb0500">100</ce:cross-ref>]. Therefore, efforts have been focused on developing candidate vaccines based on the most prevalent CFs, that is, CFA/I and CS1-CS6, which are considered to cover up to 80% of clinical ETEC isolates [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1145" refid="bb0045">9</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1150" refid="bb0505">101</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0225" view="all">An oral inactivated ETEC vaccine, ETVAX, expressing CFA/1, CS3, CS5, and CS6 in combination with an LT toxoid, has been shown to induce strong mucosal immune responses against the different CFs in adults in non-endemic countries and adults and young children in low- and middle-income countries [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1155" refid="bb0435">87</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1160" refid="bb0510">102</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1165" refid="bb0515">103</ce:cross-ref>]. The vaccine has provided significant protection against moderate–severe ETEC diarrhea in Finnish travelers to Africa [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1170" refid="bb0015">3</ce:cross-ref>], and studies are underway to evaluate protection against ETEC disease in children in Africa [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1175" refid="bb0435">87</ce:cross-ref>]. An oral live attenuated ETEC vaccine, ACE527, consisting of recombinant strains expressing CFA/I, CS1, CS2, CS3, CS5, CS6, and LTB and combined with double-mutant LT (dmLT) adjuvant, was shown to induce mucosal immune responses against all CFs and to provide significant protection against ETEC disease in a human challenge trial [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1180" refid="bb0500">100</ce:cross-ref>]. However, this vaccine’s clinical development is presently not being pursued. Alternative approaches to developing CF-based ETEC vaccines have been to construct tip-proteins of prevalent CFs, for example, CFA/I and CS17, or to produce recombinant CS6 proteins for parenteral administration [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1185" refid="bb0460">92</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1190" refid="bb0465">93</ce:cross-ref>]. Work is also in progress to construct a multiple epitope fusion antigen (MEFA) vaccine candidate consisting of a protein expressing seven different CFs and nontoxic LT and ST [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1195" refid="bb0520">104</ce:cross-ref>].</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0230" view="all">Several licensed ETEC vaccines are being developed to protect neonatal piglets; they rely on sow vaccination and passive colostrum antibody immunity. The first-generation vaccines are administered parentally to the sows based on prevalent CFs, such as F4, F5, F6, and F41, with or without LT toxoid [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1200" refid="bb0525">105</ce:cross-ref>]. Maternal ETEC vaccines protect neonatal piglets but provide insufficient protection against PWD [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1205" refid="bb0530">106</ce:cross-ref>]. A strong secretory IgA (sIgA) antibody response is required to protect against animal ETEC diarrhea [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1210" refid="bb0475">95</ce:cross-ref>]. Therefore, there is a demand for multivalent ETEC vaccines to safeguard against PWD. Ideally, these vaccines should be dispensed orally to engage the mucosal immune system, alleviating animal stress. Recently, two orally administered live vaccines (Coliprotec®F4 and Coliprotec®F4/F18) were found to protect against PWD caused by F4 and F18-positive ETEC. However, the prevalence of specific CFs is linked to geography, which complicates the development of a universal protective animal ETEC vaccine (see Outstanding questions). Therefore, the focus has shifted towards developing vaccines that include LT, STb and/or STa, and F4 (see review by Dubreuil, 2021 [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1215" refid="bb0530">106</ce:cross-ref>]).</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0235" view="all">Antibiotic resistance against ETEC in humans and the livestock industry has been identified as a major risk factor for increasing antibiotic resistance worldwide [<ce:cross-ref id="cf1220" refid="bb0535">107</ce:cross-ref>,<ce:cross-ref id="cf1225" refid="bb0540">108</ce:cross-ref>]. Therefore, developing and using effective ETEC vaccines is essential for the health and survival of humans and animals and will reduce the use of antibiotics in both sectors.</ce:para>
               </ce:section>
               <ce:section id="s0060" view="all">
                  <ce:section-title id="st0070">Concluding remarks and future perspectives</ce:section-title>
                  <ce:para id="p0240" view="all">CFs in human and animal ETEC play a pivotal role in pathogenesis and as components of candidate ETEC vaccines. Understanding the genetic organization, assembly, and regulation of different CFs is crucial for unraveling their contribution to disease development.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0245" view="all">Surveillance is essential for monitoring shifts in CF prevalence expressed by ETEC in different geographic areas as well as in different populations and over time. Such analyses may provide insights into the dynamic nature of ETEC infections as a basis for developing effective vaccines and treatment options.</ce:para>
                  <ce:para id="p0250" view="all">Recently, some ETEC CFs were identified in human and livestock ETEC strains that show similarities in CF genetic composition and binding to similar intestinal structures; however, the prevalence and distribution of such CFs remain unclear. Exploring these CFs will enhance our understanding of ETEC evolution and host adaptation. Importantly, it will shed light on the potential ability of ETEC to transmit between different hosts, thereby spreading both virulence and antibiotic-resistance determinants (see Outstanding questions)<ce:display>
                        <ce:textbox id="b0010">
                           <ce:label>Outstanding questions</ce:label>
                           <ce:alt-text role="short" id="al0035">Outstanding questions</ce:alt-text>
                           <ce:textbox-body>
                              <ce:sections>
                                 <ce:para id="p0255" view="all">Which adaptations have ETEC undergone to thrive in diverse animal and human hosts?</ce:para>
                                 <ce:para id="p0260" view="all">Are there potentially more prevalent CFs that remain undiscovered globally?</ce:para>
                                 <ce:para id="p0265" view="all">How can large-scale genomic analysis increase our understanding of ETEC across multiple hosts?</ce:para>
                                 <ce:para id="p0270" view="all">How can we effectively analyze the variation of animal-specific CFs further using sequencing data?</ce:para>
                                 <ce:para id="p0275" view="all">Can CFs be transferred between human and animal ETEC strains?</ce:para>
                                 <ce:para id="p0280" view="all">Are there shared, or conserved, regions between human and animal CFs?</ce:para>
                                 <ce:para id="p0285" view="all">Which are the mechanisms of CF propagation, and which genetic backgrounds facilitate the retention of ETEC virulence factors?</ce:para>
                              </ce:sections>
                           </ce:textbox-body>
                        </ce:textbox>
                     </ce:display>
                  </ce:para>
               </ce:section>
            </ce:sections>

                     <ce:source-text id="se0605">Johansson, M.M. et al. (2014) Porcine intestinal glycosphingolipids recognized by F6-fimbriated enterotoxigenic Escherichia coli. Microbial pathogenesis 76, 51–60</ce:source-text>
                  </ce:bib-reference>

            <ce:glossary id="gl0005" view="all">
               <ce:section-title id="st0090">Glossary</ce:section-title>
               <ce:glossary-sec id="gs0005" view="all">
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0005">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>Colonization factors (CFs)</ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0005">proteinaceous structures expressed on the outer membrane of bacteria as fimbriae, fibrillae, or afimbrial outer-membrane proteins. They mediate the binding of ETEC bacteria to the intestinal epithelium.</ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0010">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>Enterotoxigenic <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> (ETEC)</ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0010">one of many <ce:italic>E. coli</ce:italic> pathotypes that causes watery diarrhea in both humans and animals.</ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0015">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>Enterotoxins</ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0015">multiple enterotoxins, expressed by ETEC, with three main groups: LTI, LTII, and ST. LTI consists of LTh (human) and LTp (animal), and LTII (animal) consists of LTIIa, LTIIb, and LTIIc. ST can be further grouped into STa and STb (animal), where STa consists of STh (human) and STp (human and animal).</ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0020">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>
                           <ce:italic>Escherichia coli (E. coli)</ce:italic>
                        </ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0020">a species of bacteria in the family <ce:italic>Enterobacteriaceae.</ce:italic>
                     </ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0025">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>Heat-labile toxin (LT)</ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0025">an AB<ce:inf loc="post">5</ce:inf> enterotoxin expressed alone or with STa (STh or STp) or STb. LT is actively transported across the bacterial membrane via the Sec pathway. LT is accumulated in the periplasm during <ce:italic>in vitro</ce:italic> culture. Several receptors or binding structures for LT have been identified, the ganglioside GM1 being the most established one.</ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0030">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>Heat-stable toxin (ST)</ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0030">an enterotoxin secreted across the outer membrane. The human ETEC STa subtypes, STh and STp, are small nonimmunogenic proteins, 18 and 19 amino acids, respectively. STb, which is produced only by animal ETEC strains, is slightly larger, with 48 amino acids.</ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0035">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>Noncanonical antigens</ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0035">these antigens are postulated as additional virulence factors to CFs and enterotoxins expressed by ETEC. They may not be exclusively present on ETEC strains; for example, YghJ is present on commensal <ce:italic>E. coli.</ce:italic> Other factors, for example, EatA and EtpBAC, are noncanonical antigen factors distributed across ETEC.</ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
                  <ce:glossary-entry id="ge0040">
                     <ce:glossary-heading>
                        <ce:bold>Post-weaning diarrhea (PWD)</ce:bold>
                     </ce:glossary-heading>
                     <ce:glossary-def id="gd0040">post-weaning porcine diarrhea is caused by ETEC after the piglet is weaned and no longer protected with antibodies from the sow.</ce:glossary-def>
                  </ce:glossary-entry>
               </ce:glossary-sec>
            </ce:glossary>
         </tail>
      </article>
   </xocs:serial-item></xocs:doc></originalText></full-text-retrieval-response>
